214	\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across 36 conditions. RV: robustness value (minimum partial $R^2$ an unobserved confounder must have with both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and ASR to nullify the coefficient). $\eta$ $R^2_{\mathrm{tr}}$/$R^2_{\mathrm{out}}$: partial $R^2$ of $\eta_{\max}$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (treatment) and ASR (outcome). Exc.: \checkmark\ if RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$'s partial $R^2$ with treatment, indicating $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is robust to the known capability confound. RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 8/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 1/12, $K{=}5$: 3/12, $K{=}7$: 4/12).}
215	\label{tab:sensitivity}
216	\end{table}
217	
218	RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across the 36 conditions (median by $K$: 0.30 at $K{=}3$, 0.22 at $K{=}5$, 0.21 at $K{=}7$), revealing heterogeneous robustness: some conditions show $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients robust to strong confounders (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU prompt injection, RV${=}0.52$), while others are fragile (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU authority bias, RV${=}0.005$). The known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds RV in 28/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 11/12, $K{=}5$: 9/12, $K{=}7$: 8/12). The fraction of conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is robust to the $\eta_{\max}$ confound increases with panel size (1/12 $\to$ 3/12 $\to$ 4/12), paralleling the regime transition documented in \S\ref{sec:cross_k}.
219	
220	\begin{figure}[h]
221	\centering
222	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_sensitivity_contour_K5.png}
223	\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plot at $K{=}5$ (representative). $x$-axis: confounder's partial $R^2$ with treatment ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$); $y$-axis: confounder's partial $R^2$ with outcome (ASR). Contour lines show the adjusted $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under hypothetical confounders of varying strength. The diamond marks the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark; conditions where the benchmark falls inside the zero-contour indicate that the $\eta_{\max}$ confound alone could explain away $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect.}
224	\label{fig:sensitivity_contour}
225	\end{figure}
226	
227	\section{Cross-Benchmark Prediction Robustness}
228	\label{app:cross_benchmark}
229	
230	To test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$), MMLU-derived $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions across $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$. The reverse direction (ARC$\to$MMLU) achieves 10/18, with attenuation concentrated at $K{\geq}5$ (4/12), consistent with ARC's binary format producing lower MI discriminability between judge pairs. Cross-benchmark $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. The asymmetry between prediction directions further suggests that MI estimated from richer response distributions (MMLU's four-way choice) yields a more transferable diversity measure.
231	
232	\section{Pool A Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity}
233	\label{app:sensitivity_poolA}
234	
235	To test whether reducing the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ collinearity strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent signal, we repeat the Cinelli--Hazlett analysis on the capability-matched Pool~A subsample (9 models, $r{=}0.50$, 84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$). Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.
236	
237	\begin{table}[h]
238	\centering
239	\small
240	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
241	\toprule
242	\textbf{Pool / $K$} & \textbf{RV $>$ $\eta$} & \textbf{Median RV} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\
243	\midrule
244	Full / $K{=}3$ & 1/12 & 0.30 & 1/12 \\
245	Full / $K{=}5$ & 3/12 & 0.22 & 7/12 \\
246	Pool A / $K{=}3$ & 3/12 & 0.18 & --- \\
247	Pool A / $K{=}5$ & 6/12 & 0.22 & 9/12 \\
248	\bottomrule
249	\end{tabular}
250	\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity comparison: full pool vs.\ capability-matched Pool~A. RV $>$ $\eta$: conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\ $0.81$ at $K{=}5$) doubles the fraction of robust conditions at both $K$ values. Conservative count at $K{=}5$: 5/12 (excluding MMLU $\times$ authority, $R^2{\approx}0$).}
251	\label{tab:sensitivity_poolA}
252	\end{table}
253	
254	At $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\eta_{\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (5/12 conservatively), concentrated in ARC (5/6 exceeds) with limited MMLU improvement (1/6). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 6/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity partially separates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.
255	
256	\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}
257	\label{app:epsilon}
258	
259	The log-log specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\epsilon \in \{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to $\epsilon$ at $K{=}3$ (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.10$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, with three conditions exhibiting $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At $K{=}5$, all $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient directions are preserved across $\epsilon$ values (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.067$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, all $p < 0.005$), confirming that the regime transition finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), which avoids the log+$\epsilon$ transformation entirely, provides 98.6\% directional concordance with the main OLS results (71/72 direction matches) (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
```

```text
9	
10	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}
11	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ are correlated at $r{=}0.70$ ($K{=}3$) and $r{=}0.81$ ($K{=}5$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}), reflecting a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), with robustness increasing with $K$ (1/12 $\to$ 3/12 $\to$ 4/12). The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12). Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}, orange) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12; Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), versus 3/12 in the full pool. The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled designs.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}
16	\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange diamonds, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}
17	\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
21	Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains rather than the direction of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s association. Threshold voting yields $R^2{=}0.829$ (near-deterministic); Bayesian calibration reduces $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (5+/1$-$) and overall predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12; Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:panel_selection}). Recomputing MI under attack confirms stability ($\rho{\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks; position bias disrupts MI on ARC, $\rho{=}0.08$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}). The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) confirms $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse most pronounced at $K{=}3$.
22	
```

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director is coordinating a comprehensive V2 revision of an EMNLP 2026 (ARR) paper titled "The Shifting Bottleneck: How Panel Size Modulates Adversarial Vulnerability in LLM Judge Panels." This session covers:
   - **Direction vs Significance patch**: Completed — distinguish "uniformly positive direction" (coefficient sign, 12/12) from "statistically significant" (inference-method-dependent: OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12 at K=3)
   - **V2 Figure regeneration**: 3 of 4 figures completed with V2 data
   - **Fractional logit concordance update**: 93.1% → 98.6% — completed
   - **Sensitivity gradient retraction**: Confirmed already removed
   - **Appendix V2 numerical updates** (IN PROGRESS): Cinelli-Hazlett RV Pool A, Cross-benchmark IV, K_eff stability split

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - K_eff (effective independent channels via MI): uniformly positive direction 12/12, significance varies by inference method
   - η_max (weakest-link individual susceptibility): 10/12 under OLS at K=3
   - Direction (coefficient sign) vs Significance (statistical significance) — critical distinction enforced throughout
   - Wild cluster bootstrap with K-position max-p, BH FDR α=0.05
   - GAM bootstrap: 12/12 positive direction, 88.9-100% positive rate
   - Cinelli-Hazlett sensitivity analysis (omitted variable bias framework, robustness values)
   - Pool A capability-matched subsampling (9 models, r=0.50, reduces K_eff-η_max collinearity)
   - Aggregation predictability gradient: Threshold R²=0.829 → Bayesian R²=0.087
   - Fractional logit concordance: 98.6% (71/72 direction matches)
   - Paper Agent communicates via `paper_agent_post_to_director` (NOT `main_agent_post_to_director`)
   - EMNLP 2026: 8-page main text limit, unlimited references/appendix, abstract ≤200 words

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **results.tex** (MDE paragraph, L46):
     - Critical V1→V2 fix: "non-significance" language was V1 (K_eff IS significant under OLS)
     - Changed to: `The bootstrap attenuation of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE: effects below 0.14 SD cannot be reliably detected at this panel size, though the positive coefficient direction is robust across all inference methods.`

   - **introduction.tex** (L14):
     - Separated direction from significance

```text
55	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}
56	\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\eta_{\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 0/12, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 10/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the transition zone $K^* \in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$.}
57	\label{fig:regime_transition}
58	\end{figure}
59	
60	Figure~\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 10/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 4/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 10/12 at $K{=}5$ to 9/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
61	
62	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
63	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
64	
65	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant in 2/2 conditions; under adaptive attack, both predictors attenuate (4/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant); adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.275 but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains predictive power (10/12). The gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$), confirming the contrast is most pronounced in small panels.
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}
```

205	7 & MMLU & Authority bias & 0.005 & 0.200 & 0.011 & -- \\
206	7 & ARC  & Prompt inj.    & 0.355 & 0.629 & 0.004 & -- \\

214	\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across 36 conditions. RV: robustness value (minimum partial $R^2$ an unobserved confounder must have with both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and ASR to nullify the coefficient). $\eta$ $R^2_{\mathrm{tr}}$/$R^2_{\mathrm{out}}$: partial $R^2$ of $\eta_{\max}$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (treatment) and ASR (outcome). Exc.: \checkmark\ if RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$'s partial $R^2$ with treatment, indicating $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is robust to the known capability confound. RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 8/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 1/12, $K{=}5$: 3/12, $K{=}7$: 4/12).}
215	\label{tab:sensitivity}
216	\end{table}
217	
218	RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across the 36 conditions (median by $K$: 0.30 at $K{=}3$, 0.22 at $K{=}5$, 0.21 at $K{=}7$), revealing heterogeneous robustness: some conditions show $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients robust to strong confounders (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU prompt injection, RV${=}0.52$), while others are fragile (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU authority bias, RV${=}0.005$). The known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds RV in 28/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 11/12, $K{=}5$: 9/12, $K{=}7$: 8/12). The fraction of conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is robust to the $\eta_{\max}$ confound increases with panel size (1/12 $\to$ 3/12 $\to$ 4/12), paralleling the regime transition documented in \S\ref{sec:cross_k}.
219	
220	\begin{figure}[h]
221	\centering
222	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_sensitivity_contour_K5.png}
223	\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plot at $K{=}5$ (representative). $x$-axis: confounder's partial $R^2$ with treatment ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$); $y$-axis: confounder's partial $R^2$ with outcome (ASR). Contour lines show the adjusted $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under hypothetical confounders of varying strength. The diamond marks the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark; conditions where the benchmark falls inside the zero-contour indicate that the $\eta_{\max}$ confound alone could explain away $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect.}
224	\label{fig:sensitivity_contour}
225	\end{figure}
226	
227	\section{Cross-Benchmark Prediction Robustness}
228	\label{app:cross_benchmark}
229	
230	To test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$), MMLU-derived $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions across $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$. The reverse direction (ARC$\to$MMLU) achieves 10/18, with attenuation concentrated at $K{\geq}5$ (4/12), consistent with ARC's binary format producing lower MI discriminability between judge pairs. Cross-benchmark $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. The asymmetry between prediction directions further suggests that MI estimated from richer response distributions (MMLU's four-way choice) yields a more transferable diversity measure.
231	
232	\section{Pool A Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity}
233	\label{app:sensitivity_poolA}
234	
235	To test whether reducing the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ collinearity strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent signal, we repeat the Cinelli--Hazlett analysis on the capability-matched Pool~A subsample (9 models, $r{=}0.50$, 84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$). Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.
236	
237	\begin{table}[h]
238	\centering
239	\small
240	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
241	\toprule
242	\textbf{Pool / $K$} & \textbf{RV $>$ $\eta$} & \textbf{Median RV} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} \\
243	\midrule
244	Full / $K{=}3$ & 1/12 & 0.30 & 1/12 \\
245	Full / $K{=}5$ & 3/12 & 0.22 & 7/12 \\
246	Pool A / $K{=}3$ & 3/12 & 0.18 & --- \\
247	Pool A / $K{=}5$ & 6/12 & 0.22 & 9/12 \\
248	\bottomrule
249	\end{tabular}
250	\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity comparison: full pool vs.\ capability-matched Pool~A. RV $>$ $\eta$: conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\ $0.81$ at $K{=}5$) doubles the fraction of robust conditions at both $K$ values. Conservative count at $K{=}5$: 5/12 (excluding MMLU $\times$ authority, $R^2{\approx}0$).}
251	\label{tab:sensitivity_poolA}
252	\end{table}
253	
254	At $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\eta_{\max}$ confound in 6/12 conditions (5/12 conservatively), concentrated in ARC (5/6 exceeds) with limited MMLU improvement (1/6). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 6/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity partially separates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.
255	
256	\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}
257	\label{app:epsilon}
258	
259	The log-log specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\epsilon \in \{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to $\epsilon$ at $K{=}3$ (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.10$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, with three conditions exhibiting $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At $K{=}5$, all $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient directions are preserved across $\epsilon$ values (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.067$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, all $p < 0.005$), confirming that the regime transition finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), which avoids the log+$\epsilon$ transformation entirely, provides 98.6\% directional concordance with the main OLS results (71/72 direction matches) (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
260	
261	\section{Temperature Sensitivity Pilot}
262	\label{app:temperature}
263	
264	We evaluate $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ stability across decoding temperatures $t \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.7, 1.0\}$ using a 3-model pilot (GPT-4.1-nano, Gemini-3.5-Flash, Claude-3-Haiku; 300 clean samples per benchmark, 3 repeats). The maximum $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ variation across all four temperatures is $\Delta < 0.034$ (MMLU: 0.033, ARC: 0.027), confirming that decoding randomness does not meaningfully perturb pairwise MI structure. Clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computation is thus robust to temperature variation within the range used by standard API configurations.
265	
266	\section{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ Distribution Statistics}
267	\label{app:keff_dist}
268	
269	Table~\ref{tab:keff_dist} summarizes the distribution of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across panel sizes.
```

The maximum $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation across all four temperatures is $\\Delta < 0.034$ (MMLU: 0.033, ARC: 0.027), confirming that decoding randomness does not meaningfully perturb pairwise MI structure. Clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ computation is thus robust to temperature variation within the range used by standard API configurations.\n\n\\section{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ Distribution Statistics}\n\\label{app:keff_dist}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:keff_dist} summarizes the distribution of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across panel sizes.",

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{Predictor Shift Mechanism.}
5	At $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge can flip the panel, making both $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ relevant (co-determination: 3/12 and 4/12 under bootstrap). At $K{\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\geq}3$ judges; high-$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces, and $\eta_{\max}$'s marginal contribution collapses because even high-$\eta$ judges cannot flip the panel alone. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.
6	
7	\paragraph{Uniform Positive Direction.}
8	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions under both OLS and GAM bootstrap (88.9--100\% positive rate at $K{=}3$, 100\% at $K{=}5$); statistical significance depends on inference method (OLS 12/12; wild cluster bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 10/12 at $K{=}5$). A Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:bft_reversal}), ruling out attack-mechanism-dependent directional structure.
9	
10	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}
11	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ are correlated at $r{=}0.70$ ($K{=}3$) and $r{=}0.81$ ($K{=}5$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}), reflecting a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), with robustness increasing with $K$ (1/12 $\to$ 3/12 $\to$ 4/12). The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12). Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}, orange) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12; Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), versus 3/12 in the full pool. The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled designs.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}
16	\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange diamonds, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}
17	\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
21	Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains rather than the direction of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s association. Threshold voting yields $R^2{=}0.829$ (near-deterministic); Bayesian calibration reduces $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (5+/1$-$) and overall predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12; Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:panel_selection}). Recomputing MI under attack confirms stability ($\rho{\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks; position bias disrupts MI on ARC, $\rho{=}0.08$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}). The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) confirms $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse most pronounced at $K{=}3$.
22	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor Shift Mechanism.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge can flip the panel, making both $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ relevant (co-determination: 3/12 and 4/12 under bootstrap). At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces, and $\\eta_{\\max}$'s marginal contribution collapses because even high-$\\eta$ judges cannot flip the panel alone. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.\n\n\\paragraph{Uniform Positive Direction.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions under both OLS and GAM bootstrap (88.9--100\\% positive rate at $K{=}3$, 100\\% at $K{=}5$); statistical significance depends on inference method (OLS 12/12; wild cluster bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 10/12 at $K{=}5$). A Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}), ruling out attack-mechanism-dependent directional structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ are correlated at $r{=}0.70$ ($K{=}3$) and $r{=}0.81$ ($K{=}5$; Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}), reflecting a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows the known $\\eta_{\\max}$ confound exceeds $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}), with robustness increasing with $K$ (1/12 $\\to$ 3/12 $\\to$ 4/12). The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12). Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$; Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}, orange) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), versus 3/12 in the full pool. The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled designs.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange diamonds, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}\n\\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains rather than the direction of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s association. Threshold voting yields $R^2{=}0.829$ (near-deterministic); Bayesian calibration reduces $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (5+/1$-$) and overall predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Recomputing MI under attack confirms stability ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks; position bias disrupts MI on ARC, $\\rho{=}0.08$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}). The random-fault contrast (\\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) confirms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse most pronounced at $K{=}3$.\n",

Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels.\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ slightly ahead. Point estimates remain uniformly positive for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, but confidence intervals are wide at this panel size (Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (MDE decreases to 0.048 at $K{=}5$ and 0.036 at $K{=}7$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE: effects below 0.14 SD cannot be reliably detected at this panel size, though the positive coefficient direction is robust across all inference methods.\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ results above show that both predictors attenuate under corrected inference, with neither clearly dominant. A natural question is whether this co-determination persists at larger panel sizes. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 0/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 10/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the transition zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 10/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 4/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 10/12 at $K{=}5$ to 9/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant in 2/2 conditions; under adaptive attack, both predictors attenuate (4/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant); adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.275 but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains predictive power (10/12). The gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$), confirming the contrast is most pronounced in small panels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}\n\\caption{GAM bootstrap $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction at $K{=}3$ ($B{=}9999$). All 12 conditions show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (88.9--100\\% bootstrap rate). The Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$).}\n\\label{fig:gam_split}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Uniform Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap analysis confirms the uniform positive direction of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across all conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, all 12 conditions show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction with bootstrap positive rates ranging from 88.9\\% (MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation) to 100\\% (7 conditions). At $K{=}5$, all 12 conditions reach 100\\% positive rate. A Kruskal--Wallis test on GAM slope magnitudes finds no significant difference between targeted and shared-bias attack categories ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$), confirming that the positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--vulnerability association is uniform across attack mechanisms.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Bayesian $\\eta_{\\max}$ 9/12 is OLS; bootstrap validation pending.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a predictability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive direction is preserved across majority (12+/0$-$) and weighted (12+/0$-$) voting; Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) and reduces overall predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12 under OLS), yielding partial rather than complete defense.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CrFh71LWxxN7vyYbZeiq7r`</summary>

```text
appendix.tex:126:The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$) demonstrates that elevated collinearity between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ partially inflates standard errors: both predictors achieve moderate significance but neither dominates, and the transition does not replicate. Breaking collinearity through capability-matched subsampling (pool~A, $r{=}0.50$) strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s emergence to 9/12, comparable to the full-pool 10/12. Pool~B ($r{=}0.73$) falls near the decorrelation threshold, and the transition weakens accordingly. Random 9-model subsampling (1{,}000 trials) confirms an inverted-U: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance rate peaks when the subsample includes 3--4 local models (maximizing capability spread) and drops when the pool is too homogeneous (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
appendix.tex:247:Pool A / $K{=}5$ & 10/12 & 0.22 & 9/12 \\
appendix.tex:250:\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity comparison: full pool vs.\ capability-matched Pool~A. RV $>$ $\eta$: conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value exceeds the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark. Pool~A's reduced collinearity ($r{=}0.50$ vs.\ $0.81$ at $K{=}5$) increases the fraction of robust conditions from 1/12 to 2/12 at $K{=}3$ and from 3/12 to 10/12 at $K{=}5$, with the largest gains at $K{=}5$ where both benchmarks improve equally (ARC 5/6, MMLU 5/6).}
appendix.tex:254:At $K{=}5$, Pool~A RV exceeds the $\eta_{\max}$ confound in 10/12 conditions (ARC 5/6, MMLU 5/6; the two failures are both sycophancy). The improvement from full-pool 3/12 to Pool~A 10/12 demonstrates that reducing collinearity substantially separates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s signal from capability variance, though full causal identification would require training-based diversity control.
analysis.tex:11:$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ are correlated at $r{=}0.70$ ($K{=}3$) and $r{=}0.81$ ($K{=}5$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}), reflecting a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), with robustness increasing with $K$ (1/12 $\to$ 3/12 $\to$ 4/12). The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12). Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}, orange) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 10/12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), versus 3/12 in the full pool. The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled designs.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012ddzatZMyntBD1YRT5owyw`</summary>

```text
results.tex:60:Figure~\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 10/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 6/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 10/12 at $K{=}5$ to 9/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
appendix.tex:155:\caption{Spearman rank correlation between clean and attacked $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 455 panels ($K{=}3$). Four attack types preserve MI structure ($\rho{\geq}0.91$); prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$); position bias substantially disrupts pairwise dependencies.}
appendix.tex:159:Four attack types (score manipulation, authority bias, sycophancy, verbosity bias) preserve MI structure with $\rho{\geq}0.91$ on both benchmarks, validating the use of clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ as a predictor. Prompt injection shows moderate perturbation ($\rho{\approx}0.60$--$0.63$), consistent with its mechanism of overriding evaluation instructions, which can alter pairwise agreement patterns. Position bias substantially disrupts MI structure ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC, $p{=}0.10$), consistent with its anomalous regression behavior in Table~\ref{tab:dominance} (sole non-significant $\eta_{\max}$ condition) and suggesting that positional reordering fundamentally changes inter-judge dependence rather than merely introducing noise.
appendix.tex:333:$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} are near-equivalent ($\rho{=}0.94$), with comparable significance counts (8/12 vs.\ 7/12) and effect sizes (0.164 vs.\ 0.194). Disagreement rate achieves similar predictive power ($\rho{=}0.86$, 8/12). The $Q$-statistic is the weakest predictor (2/12, $|\bar{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}|{=}0.080$), consistent with its known insensitivity to the type of error correlation that drives panel vulnerability \citep{kuncheva2003measures}. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s advantage over these alternatives is not predictive power but interpretability: it quantifies the number of effective independent channels in the panel, connecting directly to the information-theoretic foundation of panel aggregation (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
Shell cwd was reset to /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/.claude/sessions/paper_agent_e719fc52

    "type": "skill_listing",
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DLYvLhnV7pKZRLktSCUAvW`</summary>

```text
appendix.tex:79:Under the most conservative inference (partial information + clustered SE), $\eta_{\max}$ remains the sole significant predictor with $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.589$, achieving significance in 12/12 conditions, stronger than the oracle baseline (8/12 under clustered SE). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ loses all predictive power (0/12). The averaging over masking scenarios smooths estimation noise inherent in the three-judge oracle computation, strengthening the $\eta_{\max}$ signal. This demonstrates that $\eta_{\max}$ computed from partial panel information is robust to incomplete composition knowledge and retains (indeed improves) its predictive power for panel ASR.
appendix.tex:94:\label{app:forest_plot}
appendix.tex:98:\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig5_forest_plot.pdf}
appendix.tex:99:\caption{Standardized coefficients with clustered SE 95\% CIs for $\eta_{\max}$ (left) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (right) across 12 conditions. Filled circles: FDR $< 0.05$. $\eta_{\max}$ shows consistent positive effects (8/12 significant); $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ CIs predominantly contain zero (0/12 significant).}
appendix.tex:100:\label{fig:forest_plot}
results.tex:37:Each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\times}$. Figure~\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ slightly ahead. Point estimates remain uniformly positive for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, but confidence intervals are wide at this panel size (Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).
results.tex:46:Power analysis yields MDE $\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\% power (MDE decreases to 0.048 at $K{=}5$ and 0.036 at $K{=}7$; Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE: effects below 0.14 SD cannot be reliably detected at this panel size, though the positive coefficient direction is robust across all inference methods.
analysis.tex:8:$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions under both OLS and GAM bootstrap (88.9--100\% positive rate at $K{=}3$, 100\% at $K{=}5$); statistical significance depends on inference method (OLS 12/12; wild cluster bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 10/12 at $K{=}5$). A Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:bft_reversal}), ruling out attack-mechanism-dependent directional structure.
main.tex:40:Our study has three principal limitations. First, the combinatorial panel design introduces non-independence; we address this with wild cluster bootstrap ($K$-position max-$p$), but the correction grows more conservative at larger $K$, partly explaining the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ attenuation from 10/12 ($K{=}5$) to 9/12 ($K{=}7$). At $K{=}3$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s statistical significance is particularly sensitive to inference method (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 4/12), though coefficient direction remains uniformly positive. Second, both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats whose attack surface differs structurally from open-ended evaluation; the six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization. Whether the predictor shift holds for open-ended or full-precision settings is unknown. Third, we study only odd $K \in \{3,5,7\}$; the transition threshold $K^*$ ($3 < K^* < 5$) is not precisely identified, and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent contribution remains only partially separable from co-occurring capability variance (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
Shell cwd was reset to /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/.claude/sessions/paper_agent_e719fc52

```text
33	\end{table}
34	
35	Under OLS, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\eta_{\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\eta_{\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels.
36	
37	Each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\times}$. Figure~\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ slightly ahead. Point estimates remain uniformly positive for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, but confidence intervals are wide at this panel size (Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).
38	
39	\begin{figure}[t]
40	\centering
41	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}
42	\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}
43	\label{fig:robustness}
44	\end{figure}
45	
46	Power analysis yields MDE $\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\% power (MDE decreases to 0.048 at $K{=}5$ and 0.036 at $K{=}7$; Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE: effects below 0.14 SD cannot be reliably detected at this panel size, though the positive coefficient direction is robust across all inference methods.
47	
48	\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}
49	\label{sec:cross_k}
50	
51	The $K{=}3$ results above show that both predictors attenuate under corrected inference, with neither clearly dominant. A natural question is whether this co-determination persists at larger panel sizes. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, constructing all $\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.
52

256	\section{Epsilon Sensitivity Analysis}
257	\label{app:epsilon}
258	
259	The log-log specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. We assess sensitivity by comparing coefficients across $\epsilon \in \{10^{-3}, 10^{-6}, 10^{-9}\}$. Zero ASR rates are low on MMLU ($<$1\% of panels across conditions) but can reach 38--40\% on ARC for authority bias and sycophancy at $K{=}7$. Coefficient magnitudes show moderate sensitivity to $\epsilon$ at $K{=}3$ (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.10$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, with three conditions exhibiting $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sign changes), reflecting the influence of zero-ASR panels on the log transformation. At $K{=}5$, all $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient directions are preserved across $\epsilon$ values (maximum $\Delta\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.067$ between $\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ and $\epsilon{=}10^{-9}$, all $p < 0.005$), confirming that the regime transition finding is robust to this modeling choice. The fractional logit specification (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), which avoids the log+$\epsilon$ transformation entirely, provides 98.6\% directional concordance with the main OLS results (71/72 direction matches) (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).
260	
261	\section{Temperature Sensitivity Pilot}
262	\label{app:temperature}
263	
264	We evaluate $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ stability across decoding temperatures $t \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.7, 1.0\}$ using a 3-model pilot (GPT-4.1-nano, Gemini-3.5-Flash, Claude-3-Haiku; 300 clean samples per benchmark, 3 repeats). The maximum $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ variation across all four temperatures is $\Delta < 0.034$ (MMLU: 0.033, ARC: 0.027), confirming that decoding randomness does not meaningfully perturb pairwise MI structure. Clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computation is thus robust to temperature variation within the range used by standard API configurations.
265	
```

The bootstrap attenuation of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE: effects below 0.14 SD cannot be reliably detected at this panel size, though the positive coefficient direction is robust across all inference methods.\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ results above show that both predictors attenuate under corrected inference, with neither clearly dominant. A natural question is whether this co-determination persists at larger panel sizes. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 0/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 10/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the transition zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 10/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 6/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 10/12 at $K{=}5$ to 9/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant in 2/2 conditions; under adaptive attack, both predictors attenuate (4/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant); adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.275 but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains predictive power (10/12). The gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$), confirming the contrast is most pronounced in small panels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}\n\\caption{GAM bootstrap $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction at $K{=}3$ ($B{=}9999$). All 12 conditions show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (88.9--100\\% bootstrap rate). The Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$).}\n\\label{fig:gam_split}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Uniform Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap analysis confirms the uniform positive direction of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across all conditions (Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, all 12 conditions show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction with bootstrap positive rates ranging from 88.9\\% (MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation) to 100\\% (7 conditions). At $K{=}5$, all 12 conditions reach 100\\% positive rate. A Kruskal--Wallis test on GAM slope magnitudes finds no significant difference between targeted and shared-bias attack categories ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$), confirming that the positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--vulnerability association is uniform across attack mechanisms.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Bayesian $\\eta_{\\max}$ 9/12 is OLS; bootstrap validation pending.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a predictability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive direction is preserved across majority (12+/0$-$) and weighted (12+/0$-$) voting; Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) and reduces overall predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12 under OLS), yielding partial rather than complete defense.\n",
